#### ***Applications of IoT***

• Can help in food transportation procurement using RFID technology. Among the IoT tools to track food products, RFID technology is the most effective and expensive \[[13](#9789815274349-C6.html#r13)\].

• Can help in the production and improvement of food safety

• Helps in providing facilitation to food manufactures with accurate prediction of future issues.

• Helps in maintaining the integrity of perishable food if worked smartly, as some products require special preservation.

• Helps in improving logistics.

• Helps in food authenticity by providing digital identification marks (digital tags) on any given product.

• Helps in monitoring mechanical damage in any machine, which directly helps in the quality of food.

• Helps in demand prediction according to the preference of consumer.

• Helps in locating goods in the warehouse, which in turn improves staff safety.

• Helps in monitoring storage conditions through environmental sensors, which can make changes accordingly in shipping circumstances.

• Reduces dependency on human data entry or conventional inventory devices.

#### IoT in the Food Supply Chain

Nowadays, there are large numbers of top trending technologies for various day-to-day activities. The **Internet of Things** is also among these technologies, which has been playing its role in various activities. Since India’s large population depends on agriculture for its survival, we need to find the role of this technology in the food supply chain \[[14](#9789815274349-C6.html#r14)\].

**IoT** is rapidly transforming the food supply, improving transparency and ensuring the safety of the food that we eat. It offers various benefits that can help in ensuring the quality and safety of food products throughout the supply chain. The major benefit of IoT is improved transparency. It has made it easy for companies to trace the source of food and monitor its progress through the supply chain by enabling tracking solutions that help in identifying problems and issues with food supply at any stage. Various sensors and devices can help monitor food quality, temperature, and other environmental factors, ensuring food safety requirements.

This technology can also help consumers by giving detailed information about the food they buy since companies are able to trace the source of the food. It can affect how everything works, including how food is produced, processed, stored, distributed, and consumed on farms.

### The architecture of IoT in the food supply chain

• **Measurement layer** data deals with time, location, temperature, machine, and other information.

• **Network layer** gathers data at the sensor layer and communicates it to the service layer by means of Wireless Sensor Networks (WSNs), Bluetooth, and Wi-Fi routers.

• **Service layer** contain**s** a variety of analytics engines/tools and services where data is stored/kept.

• **Application layer** is made up of several modules and programs (e.g., production effectiveness, food quality).

### Widely accepted definitions of Blockchain

**1)** “Blockchain is a shared, immutable ledger that facilitates the process of recording transactions and tracking assets in a business network. An *asset* can be tangible (a house, car, cash and land) or intangible (intellectual property, patents, copyrights, branding). Virtually anything of value can be tracked and traded on a Blockchain network, reducing risk and cutting costs for all involved” \[[15](#9789815274349-C6.html#r15)\].

**2)** Blockchain technology is a decentralized, distributed ledger \[[16](#9789815274349-C6.html#r16)\].

**3)** Blockchain is a secure database shared across a network of participants \[[17](#9789815274349-C6.html#r17)\].

From the above definitions, it can be concluded that Blockchain is:

1) **A Shared Platform**\- The chain is shared among the network of members involved.

2) **De-centralized and Distributed ledger**\- A digital ledger that can be viewed by a permitted audience, but no one person has absolute authority over the data.

3) **Immutable**\- No data can be deleted or modified without the concern (acceptance) of at least 51% of the members in the network.

4) **Secure**\- Since the ledger is distributed over a very large number of computers, it is impossible for a hacker to hack every single computer connected to the network.

### Looking back at Blockchain

Cryptographically secured transactions have been the basis of the Blockchain concept since 1991 when Stuart Haber and Wakefield Scott Stornetta introduced it. There was, however, not much development until 2008. In 2008, Satoshi Nakamoto (a pseudonym) created the first child of this technology, which gained traction and widespread usage. The first cryptocurrency using BCT, named Bitcoin, was launched in 2009, paving the way for Blockchain's impact in the tech world ([Table **5**](#9789815274349-C6.html#t5)).

Table 5 **Timeline of milestone events in Blockchain development.**  

| **S. No.** || **Milestone Events** || **Year** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 || Stuart Haber and Wakefield Scott Stornetta proposed a cryptographically safe chain of records called blocks. || 1991 |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 2 || Satoshi Nakamoto devised the technology's concept and anticipated applications, and the first Blockchain and cryptocurrency were formally introduced. || 2008 |
| 3 || IBM revealed a Blockchain plan for cloud-based business solutions || 2017 |
| 4 || The Japanese government acknowledged the legitimacy of Blockchain and  
cryptocurrency. || 2018 |
| 5 || Dubai declared that its government would be powered by Blockchain by 2020. || 2019 |
| 6 || Facebook promised to launch its BCT || 2020 |

  

### Characteristics of Blockchain

1) Digital assets are disseminated rather than copied or transmitted.

2) Digital assets can be accessed and governed and are transparent in real-time by several parties due to their distributed and decentralized nature.

3) All changes to the Blockchain ledger are recorded, maintaining the integrity and security of the distributed ledger.

4) Due to the fact that Blockchain ledgers are public and are built with inherent security safeguards, they are an ideal solution for practically any sector.

### Application of Blockchain in Agriculture

1) Transparent transaction management

2) Efficient financing

3) Improved financial support for developing countries

4) Efficient smart farm contracts

5) Agriculture Insurance: Safety net for farmers

6) Land registries

7) Monitoring data

### Blockchain for Food Supply Chain Management

A food supply chain is a complicated system of activities and procedures that include multiple phases, such as food product manufacturing, processing, packaging, distribution, and consumption. It encompasses all of the actions involved in the transfer of food items from the farm to the end user's plate.

Trying to control the food supply chain can provide a slew of challenges. Food safety, quality control, supply chain interruptions, and environmental sustainability may be among the issues. As a result, there is an increasing need for BCT to increase transparency, traceability, and efficiency in the food supply chain.

### Blockchain

The use of BCT may be useful in managing food supply chains by facilitating a transparent and secure system for tracking the movement of food products from their origin to their destination. Here are some ways in which Blockchain can be used to manage food supply chains:

1) **Traceability**: As a result of the use of the Blockchain, it is possible to track food items from the farmer to the consumer in a tamper-proof manner by keeping a record of all the transactions that occur in the supply chain. When a food safety issue arises, this information can also be utilized to identify the source of contamination or spoilage.

2) **Transparency**: Blockchain is a distributed ledger, which means the database is shared among all the stakeholders. This ensures utmost transparency and smooth communication between stakeholders and makes management easy.

3) **Quality control**: A definite quality standard can be set, and a digital record of the quality of products can be maintained at various stages of production, storage, and transportation. This ensures that only those food items that match the previous set quality standard reach the end consumer.

4) **Efficiency**: Blockchain can also help in automating inventory management, order tracking, and financial transactions to save costs and improve efficiency.

5) **Sustainability**: Blockchain can help reduce food wastage by creating a system of tracking, conserving resources, and promoting sustainable production practices.

Blockchain technology can provide a powerful tool for managing food supply chains and improving efficiency, transparency, and accountability across the entire supply chain.

### Any Food Supply Chain Starts with the following:

1) **The producer (farmer)**: The information that can be recorded during this stage is altitude, irrigation treatment, temperature, humidity, soil fertility, date of harvesting, and dispatching to the next link in the chain. Legal certificates like Organic Farming Certificates (if any) are also recorded at this stage.

2) **Food processors**: Information that is recorded at this stage may include location, receiving date, processes, storage and packaging details, shelf life, temperature, date of dispatch, etc.

3) **The distributor**: Information that is recorded at this stage is again similar to the previous link, like location, receiving date, inventory details, storage condition, information of retailers and wholesalers, and finally, dispatched date to the next link.

4) **Retailer:** The information recorded in this stage are location, receiving date, and sales information. When the product is sold to the end consumer, the sale information is also stored so that no other sale on the same label can be made.

5) **The end consumer:** They are the final link in the food supply chain; thus, they have the full right to know every detail about the food they are consuming. This is possible with the help of Blockchain, where the authenticity, origin, and journey can be visible to the end consumer.

### Genesis and History of Public Distribution System (PDS)

Food shortages during World War II led to the conception of the PDS in 1942 in India, where it evolved into an ultramodern program (Fig. [**2**](#9789815274349-C6.html#f2)). As a part of the British government's effort to ensure equitable distribution of food to civic consumers after high prices in 1939, rationing was instituted by Bombay in 1939 \[[18](#9789815274349-C6.html#r18)\]. Consequently, the policy of stabilizing and operating food grains has its roots in the practice of rationing during times of extreme hardship, particularly during times of shortage. The PDS was designed to ensure that essential supplies are readily accessible to consumers everywhere in the country. In this system, foodgrains, sugar, and other necessities such as kerosene oil and edible oil are made available to the citizens of the state at fair prices in order to meet their basic needs. As a way to keep inflationary tendencies under control, a close monitoring system is necessary to make PDS an effective tool against numerous influences on the open market \[[18](#9789815274349-C6.html#r18)\]. Targeted Public Distribution System (TPDS) was introduced by the Indian government in June 1997 as a means of providing assistance to the underprivileged. It was mandated that states develop and implement plans to identify the poor in order to distribute food grains and to distribute those grains at the Fair Price Shop (FPS) level in an ethical and responsible manner (National Food Security Portal, 2023) \[19\].

### Evolution of PDS in India

![](Images/9789815274349-C6-F2.jpg)**Fig. (2))**  
Timeline of PDS in India. 

### Public Distribution System (PDS) in India

A key component of the government's program for managing the nation's food economy, the PDS evolved as a mechanism for managing food shortages by distributing foodgrains at a low cost (Fig. [**3**](#9789815274349-C6.html#f3)). Rice, wheat, sugar, and kerosene are distributed to states and UTs for distribution under the PDS \[[20](#9789815274349-C6.html#r20)\].

![](Images/9789815274349-C6-F3.jpg)**Fig. (3))**  
Importance of PDS \[[20](#9789815274349-C6.html#r20)\]. 

### What is the current state of using modern ICT in the food sector?

Food security is provided to 81.35 crore citizens each month through the Targeted Public Distribution System (TPDS) ([Table **6**](#9789815274349-C6.html#t6)). This program is regulated by the National Food Security Act (NFSA, 2013). In order to modernize and reform the public distribution system through technology, the government is currently implementing the SMART-PDS scheme. A great deal of data will be generated in this project that can be used to enhance the delivery of other government welfare programs and schemes. According to the Food Ministry, the SMART-PDS program is expected to cost approximately 350 crores, which will be divided among the states and implemented over a 3 years period between 2023 and 2025.

Table 6 **Key challenges in the PDS supply chain.**  

| **Problems** || **Cause** || **Effect** || **References** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Movement of food grain || Concentration of procurement activities in a small number of states despite PDS's nationwide operations || Costly logistics for shipping, a delay in delivery, and insufficient control over stock monitoring || Banerjee (2011) \[[21](#9789815274349-C6.html#r21)\] |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Excess cost || Low monitoring and control;  
high administrative  
cost || Higher government subsidy burden || Ahluwalia (1993); Jha & Ramaswami (2010) \[[22](#9789815274349-C6.html#r22)\] |
| Concentration in certain crops || Price stabilization mechanism developed through Minimum Support Price (MSP) || Reserved commodity surpluses reduce the availability of other commodities. || Ramaswami (2002) \[[23](#9789815274349-C6.html#r23)\] |
| Speculation in the market || High procurement and later selling them on the open market || Decreases private stocks, which raises consumer prices on the open market and creates uncertainty about how to manage the demand-supply imbalance. || Ramaswami (2002) \[[23](#9789815274349-C6.html#r23)\] |

  

### What is SMART-PDS?

In order to improve the efficiency, transparency, and accountability of the country’s targeted Public Distribution System (TPDS), the Indian government launched the SMART-PDS (Scheme for Modernization and Reform through Technology in Public Distribution System), as illustrated in (Fig. [**4**](#9789815274349-C6.html#f4)) \[[24](#9789815274349-C6.html#r24)\].

![](Images/9789815274349-C6-F4.jpg)**Fig. (4))**  
Objectives of the SMART-PDS initiative. 

### “Food for all Model” of Chhattisgarh

In Chhattisgarh, PDS coverage is universal as a result of the coexistence of the National Food Security Act, 2003 (NFSA) and the Chhattisgarh Food Security Act, 2012 \[[25](#9789815274349-C6.html#r25)\]. An innovation in Chhattisgarh is the implementation of an ICT-enabled Universal Public Distribution System (UPDS) that is designed to ensure “Food For All” and ensure a variety of dimensions of food security, including accessibility, utilization, stability, and agency. As a result of the political context, Chhattisgarh's food security programs have been designed with practical and applicable technological solutions.

• Through the deployment of the ICT-driven Centralized Online Real-time Electronic CORE-PDS in 2011-2012, the UPDS has become accessible and movable to the state's beneficiaries. As a general rule, the CORE-PDS is an online distribution system, but there will be some limited offline deals in the event of temporary connectivity issues at Fair Price Shops.

• By authenticating themselves through any of the multiple instruments permitted, beneficiaries in the CORE-PDS can access their portions from any state that has notified FPS. It is interesting to know that the technological architecture of the RSBY scheme is designed in such a manner that little effort is required for its expansion to other social security schemes as it is prepared to include the necessary information for benefit delivery to other schemes \[[26](#9789815274349-C6.html#r26)\] i.e., Smart Ration Card/PDS. In addition, the robust technology platform of RSBY has contributed to standardizing operations among all stakeholders.

• As the FPS salesperson inserts the smart card in the electronic Point of Sales (ePoS) device, the number on the smart card is read and transmitted to the server via General Packet Radio Services (GPRS) for the purpose of obtaining beneficiary entitlement balances.

• Upon receiving the beneficiary's amount, the FPS salesman enters the details of the sale and submits them to the system. An update to the sale is made by the server, which generates a success report. The beneficiaries receive a receipt in addition to the goods or food grains.

### Centralized Online Real-Time Electronic Public Distribution System (CORE-PDS) of Chhattisgarh in India

A major objective of the Indian Government is to eliminate black marketing and improve distribution efficiency through the computerization of more than 50% of fair shops by March 2017 \[[27](#9789815274349-C6.html#r27)\]. In 2007, Chhattisgarh was the first state to computerize the PDS supply chain, and in 2012, it introduced the Centralized Online Real-Time Electronic Public Distribution System (CORE-PDS), which has become the model for other states. A forced monopoly was established by the owners of the fair price shops when each beneficiary household was tied to a particular fair price shop. Because fair price shops competed with one another for customers by offering better services in order to avoid losing them, every beneficiary could select the fair price shop from which s/he would purchase their rations. ICT was employed to create an effective and transparent system. Thus, all fair price shops have Point of Sale (POS) systems with internet access, and smart cards are provided to recipients. A POS device is used to swipe a smart card to verify eligibility after the salesperson inputs the desired quantity of commodities, which is updated with the central server. A gadget generates a receipt that is presented to the beneficiary after the payment is accepted and the beneficiary receives the commodity (Fig. [**5**](#9789815274349-C6.html#f5)).

![](Images/9789815274349-C6-F5.jpg)**Fig. (5))**  
Cyclic pentagonal model of chhattisgarh PDS system. 

### Phases of Chhattisgarh’s Model

It became a model state in the Public Distribution System and was also praised by the union government and Supreme Court, which ordered other states to follow its model \[[28](#9789815274349-C6.html#r28)\]. The state was able to do all this in 3 distinct phases:

• **First phase:** The state government brought a number of changes to PDS delivery and procurement by eliminating the middlemen, de-privatizing all ration shops, ensuring the establishment of ration shops in each gram panchayat, and providing ration to cardholders.

• **Second phase:** To bring more transparency, efforts were increased, the public was involved, and the system was made online. For this, call centres were established, and all the information was available on the website regarding the collection and distribution of rations.

• **Third phase:** The procurement process of paddy was strengthened, and an online system was also introduced for this. Also, arrangements were made to transfer payments to farmers’ bank accounts directly.

## Conclusion and Future Perspectives

With the use of internet technology, the food supply chain is able to use dynamic permutation in food management operations. The use of these techniques can help in making the food supply chain a more transparent system, which will help the government as well as the consumers. This can help in the collection of real-time data, and analyzing this data becomes easier, which automatically leads to an improved food supply chain. We need to educate people more about these technologies for the full use of such things. Today, AI, IoT and BCT are conspicuous everywhere, from economics to medical science and from engineering to Agriculture. IoT uses RFID technology, which tracks product locations throughout the entire supply chain, as it eliminates stock-outs in the supply chain. In BCT, data of transactions is stored in blocks with the hash (encrypted secured information) of that block and the hash of the previous block. Any attempt to change the data will result in the changing of the hash number, which will automatically be denied due to a mismatch with the hash, which makes it tamper-proof. Chhattisgarh’s PDS model has become a model state in the Public Distribution System and was also praised by the union government and Supreme Court, which ordered other states to follow its model. But there are specific shortcomings of the Chhattisgarh model, which may be addressed by the incorporation of AI, IoT and BCT. Furthermore, for truly implementing ONORC (One Nation One Ration Card) without flaw, the application of AI, IoT and BCT is quintessential.

# Food Supply Chain Management by Leveraging AI, IoT, and Blockchain Technologies

  
  
**K. Radhika^([1](#9789815274349-C7.html#aff1), [\*](#9789815274349-C7.html#cor1))****,** **Sheena Mohammed^([1](#9789815274349-C7.html#aff1))****,** **Satya Kiranmai Tadepalli^([1](#9789815274349-C7.html#aff1))****,** **Kiranmaie Puvvula^([1](#9789815274349-C7.html#aff1))**  
¹ Chaitanya Bharathi Institute of Technology, Hyderabad, India  
  

## Abstract

Traceability is an important component of food supply chain management because it allows businesses to track food products from their origin to the point of consumption. However, the current food supply chain system frequently lacks proper traceability methods, making it difficult to pinpoint the source of food safety concerns, monitor product quality, and assure regulatory compliance. As a result, there is a growing need to rethink food supply chain management to assure traceability. Artificial intelligence (AI), the Internet of Things (IoT), and blockchain are contributing to the resolution of these issues by increasing accountability, traceability, and efficiency. The application of blockchain, IoT, and AI to food supply chain management is examined in this book chapter. It highlights the possible advantages of incorporating these technologies into the food industry, including improved food safety, real-time food quality monitoring, and increased supply chain visibility. It looks at other uses as well, such as decentralized food traceability systems, smart packaging, and predictive analytics. The chapter also discusses the obstacles and restrictions that come with implementing these technologies in the food supply chain, as well as potential solutions and effectiveness.

**Keywords:** Consumer trust, Donsumption, Data analytics, Distribution, Efficiency, Food safety risks, Inventory management, Market dynamics, Quality control, Real-time monitoring, Regulatory compliance, Traceability, Transparency, Transportation, Visibility.  
  

* * *

^(\*) **Corresponding author K. Radhika:** Chaitanya Bharathi Institute of Technology, Hyderabad, India; Tel: 9346253642; E-mail: [kradhika\_aids@cbit.ac.in](mailto:kradhika_aids@cbit.ac.in)  
  

## INTRODUCTION

Food supply chain management \[[1](#9789815274349-C7.html#r1)\] refers to the process of overseeing and managing the flow of food products from the point of origin to the consumer. It encompasses all the activities involved in the production, processing, storage, transportation, and distribution of food items \[[2](#9789815274349-C7.html#r2)\]. The food supply chain shown in Fig. ([**1**](#9789815274349-C7.html#f1)) consists of several interconnected stages.

![](Images/9789815274349-C7-F1.jpg)**Fig. (1))**  
Supply chain management. 

**Procurement:** This involves sourcing raw materials, such as agricultural produce, ingredients, and packaging materials, from suppliers, farmers, or other sources.

**Processing:** Raw materials are transformed into finished food products through various manufacturing and processing operations. This stage may include cleaning, sorting, cutting, cooking, packaging, and labeling.

**Storage:** Processed food products are stored in warehouses or distribution centers before they are transported to retailers or consumers. Proper storage conditions, such as temperature control, are crucial to maintain product quality and safety.

**Transportation:** Food products are moved from storage facilities to distribution centers, retailers, or directly to consumers. This stage requires efficient logistics management, including route optimization, vehicle selection, and compliance with food safety and transportation regulations.

**Distribution:** Food products are distributed to various points of sale, such as grocery stores, restaurants, and institutions. This may involve working with wholesalers, distributors, or direct delivery to retailers. Inventory management, order fulfillment, and demand forecasting are essential aspects of distribution.

**Retail:** Food products are made available to consumers through retail outlets, both physical stores and online platforms. Retailers play a significant role in product placement, pricing, marketing, and ensuring a positive consumer experience.

**Consumption:** The final stage occurs when consumers purchase and consume the food products. Consumer feedback and preferences play a crucial role in shaping the supply chain and product development.

Food supply chain management involves coordinating and managing activities across all these stages to ensure the efficient and timely delivery of safe and quality food products \[[3](#9789815274349-C7.html#r3)\]. It requires collaboration and information sharing among various stakeholders, including suppliers, manufacturers, distributors, retailers, and consumers.

Effective food supply chain management is crucial for meeting the demands of a growing population, ensuring food security, minimizing waste, and promoting sustainability. It involves addressing challenges such as food safety risks, quality control, inventory management, traceability, regulatory compliance, and responding to market dynamics.

Advancements in technology, such as AI, IoT, and blockchain, have the potential to optimize and streamline food supply chain management by improving visibility, traceability, efficiency, and transparency \[[4](#9789815274349-C7.html#r4)\]. These technologies enable real-time monitoring, data analytics, automation, and secure data sharing, leading to enhanced operational effectiveness and consumer trust \[[5](#9789815274349-C7.html#r5)\].

## ARTIFICIAL INTELLIGENCE IN FOOD SUPPLY CHAIN MANAGEMENT

Artificial intelligence is the imitation of human intellect in computers that have been designed to do tasks that would ordinarily require human intelligence. It comprises developing computer systems capable of performing activities such as speech recognition, decision-making, problem-solving, and learning, among others. Machine learning, neural networks, natural language processing, and expert systems are some of the approaches employed by AI technology. Machine learning, a subtype of AI, feeds large amounts of data to algorithms in order for them to learn patterns, make predictions, and improve performance over time. Neural networks, which mimic the structure of the human brain, are used to process and analyze massive volumes of data. AI has found applications in a wide range of industries, including healthcare, banking, transportation, manufacturing, and others \[[6](#9789815274349-C7.html#r6)\].

### Role of AI in Food Supply Chain Management

Artificial intelligence has the capability of processing and analyzing massive volumes of data, generating correct forecasts, and automating decision-making processes, considerably improving efficiency, quality control, and overall performance in the food supply chain \[[7](#9789815274349-C7.html#r7)\]. The AI-based food supply chain tasks shown in Fig. ([**2**](#9789815274349-C7.html#f2)) are described below:

![](Images/9789815274349-C7-F2.jpg)**Fig. (2))**  
AI-enabled tasks in the food supply chain. 

**Demand Forecasting:** The power of AI can be leveraged to forecast future demand for food goods. AI analyzes historical sales data, market dynamics, weather patterns, and other relevant parameters to predict the expected demand. This helps in the optimization of production levels, inventory management, and distribution planning in order to satisfy consumer demand while minimizing waste and stock-outs.

**Inventory Management**: AI algorithms optimize inventory levels by taking into account various factors such as demand projections, lead times, and storage capacity. AI can update reorder points, safety stock levels, and replenishment schedules dynamically to guarantee effective inventory management while lowering carrying costs and decreasing the risk of stock-outs or overstocking.

**Quality Control and Inspection:** AI-driven computer vision systems that analyze visual data can be utilized to examine food contamination, damage, quality, and safety. AI-assisted quality control and inspection ensure adherence to quality standards by automating the inspection process, enhancing overall product integrity and lowering the probability of providing substandard items.

**Supply-Chain Optimization:** AI optimizes supply chain operations by analyzing data on routing paths to and from storage facilities, manufacturing schedules, and market demand. Artificial intelligence systems can identify bottlenecks, offer appropriate routes, and dynamically change production and distribution plans to reduce costs as well as improve delivery times.

**Shelf Life Prediction:** AI algorithms can analyze data such as temperature, humidity, and storage conditions to anticipate the remaining shelf life of perishable food products. This helps to optimize inventory rotation, improve the freshness of food, and reduce wastage.

**Supplier and Vendor Management:** AI can be incorporated to monitor supplier performance, evaluate vendor dependability, and identify possible risks in the supply chain. AI assists in making informed decisions on supplier selection, contract negotiations, and risk mitigation methods by analyzing data on delivery timeframes and quality issues.

### Predictive Analytics for Demand Forecasting

Predictive analytics for demand forecasting is an approach that makes accurate forecasts about future demand for products or services using historical data, statistical algorithms, and machine learning techniques. It entails analyzing patterns, trends, and factors that drive demand in order to provide projections that may guide supply chain decision-making and planning \[[8](#9789815274349-C7.html#r8)\]. The method is depicted in Fig. ([**3**](#9789815274349-C7.html#f3)).

Data collection is the initial stage in predictive analytics, and it involves gathering relevant historical data on product demand. Sales data, consumer behavior data, market trends, promotional efforts, and external elements such as weather or economic indicators can all be included. The data should be collected over a long enough period to capture seasonal fluctuations, trends, and patterns. Data preprocessing involves cleaning and transformation of the collected data into an appropriate format for analysis. This includes eliminating outliers, dealing with missing numbers, normalizing the data, and aggregating it into relevant time periods (for example, weekly or monthly).

Feature selection is the process of identifying important traits or factors that may influence demand. Historical sales data, price information, marketing efforts, competition data, and other elements that have proven an association with demand patterns might be included.

![](Images/9789815274349-C7-F3.jpg)**Fig. (3))**  
Predictive analytics process for demand forecasting. 

Model creation is to develop a demand forecasting model using several statistical and machine learning algorithms on the preprocessed data. Regression analysis, time series analysis and machine learning techniques (*e.g.*, random forest, neural networks) are all popular models. The model is trained on historical data in order to capture trends and correlations between attributes and demand.

Model evaluation and selection involves the assessment of the constructed model by comparing its anticipated outcomes to real demand data from a validation set. To test the model's correctness, performance metrics such as mean absolute error (MAE), root mean square error (RMSE), and mean absolute percentage error (MAPE) are generated. Multiple models can be tried, and the one with the best performance is chosen.

The authors of study \[[9](#9789815274349-C7.html#r9)\] suggested a novel demand forecasting model for the hospitality sector that uses Attention-Long Short Term Memory (Attention-LSTM) to anticipate weekly hotel demand four weeks in advance. This approach uses time series demand data in conjunction with additional characteristics derived from K-Means Clustering discoveries, such as the Top 10 Hotel characteristics, and achieves a mean absolute error (MAE) of 4.61 and a mean absolute percentage error (MAPE) of 23.97.

Two LSTM-based models for demand forecasting in cancer palliative care \[[10](#9789815274349-C7.html#r10)\]are the individual-based model and the population-based model. The individual-based model predicts the type and timing of services required for a specific patient based on demographic and health profiles. The population-based model forecasts the overall demand for various services in a care center, helping with resource and operations planning. These models were suggested to estimate demand for a variety of palliative care treatments for patients hospitalized at home with advanced cancer. The individual-based model predicts when a patient will require a service and what sorts of services will be required at that time. The population-based model predicts the number of services that will be sought in the future week for a service center. In forecasting the types of services required by a patient, the individual-based model had an average accuracy of 69.75% and an F1 score of 66.8%. The average MAE of forecasts for institutions No. 1 and No. 2 in the population-based model is 1.07 and 0.6, respectively. Demand forecasts help to optimize inventory management, production scheduling, resource allocation, and supply chain management approaches.

### AI-enabled Quality Control and Inspection

The process of AI-driven quality control and inspection in the food supply chain involves utilizing artificial intelligence (AI) technologies, particularly computer vision, to automate and enhance the inspection and assessment of food products for quality, safety, and compliance. A step-by-step explanation of the process is shown in Fig. ([**4**](#9789815274349-C7.html#f4)).

**Image/Video Capture:** The process begins with capturing images or videos of food products using cameras or specialized imaging devices. These images can be taken at various stages of the supply chain, such as during production, processing, packaging, or before shipment.

**Preprocessing:** The captured images/videos undergo preprocessing to enhance image quality, remove noise, and normalize them for consistent analysis. This step helps to ensure that the images are in a suitable format for AI algorithms to process.

**Training Data Collection:** A large dataset of labeled images is collected to train the AI model. These labeled images serve as examples for the AI system to learn from and understand different quality attributes, defects, or safety concerns in food products. The dataset is annotated with labels indicating good or acceptable products, as well as specific defects or quality issues.

![](Images/9789815274349-C7-F4.jpg)**Fig. (4))**  
AI-enabled Quality Control and Inspection Process. 

**Model Development:** AI models, particularly deep learning-based neural networks, are trained using the collected dataset. The models learn to identify and classify different quality attributes, defects, or safety concerns in food products based on the patterns and features observed in the training data. Multiple layers within the neural network help in extracting relevant features from the images.

**Model Validation and Optimization:** The trained model is validated using a separate dataset to assess its performance. Performance metrics such as accuracy, precision, recall, and F1 score are calculated to evaluate how well the model can identify and classify different quality issues. The model may undergo optimization and fine-tuning to improve its accuracy and robustness.

**Real-Time Inspection:** Once the model is validated and optimized, it can be deployed for real-time inspection of food products. New images or videos of food products are inputted into the AI system, which analyzes the visual data using the trained model. The model detects and identifies various quality attributes, defects, or safety concerns present in the products.

**Decision-Making and Action:** Based on the inspection results, decisions can be made regarding the acceptability of food products. If a defect or quality issue is detected, appropriate actions can be taken, such as segregating the product for further inspection, rejecting it, or triggering a quality control process to rectify the issue. The AI system can provide real-time feedback to operators or trigger alerts for immediate attention.

### AI-enabled route optimization and logistics

AI-enabled route optimization and logistics in food supply chain management involves leveraging artificial intelligence (AI) technologies to optimize the movement of goods, streamline transportation routes, and enhance overall logistics operations. By applying AI-enabled route optimization and logistics in food supply chain management, businesses can achieve several benefits, including reduced transportation costs and minimized delays. The steps in AI based route optimization techniques shown in Fig. ([**5**](#9789815274349-C7.html#f5)) are described below.

![](Images/9789815274349-C7-F5.jpg)**Fig. (5))**  
Steps in AI-enabled Route Optimization and Logistics. 

**Data Collection:** Relevant data is collected from various sources, including historical shipment data, real-time traffic information, weather conditions, delivery constraints, and customer demands. This data provides insights into transportation patterns, bottlenecks, and other factors that impact logistics operations.

**Data Analysis:** AI algorithms analyze the collected data to identify patterns, trends, and correlations. This analysis helps uncover hidden relationships between variables, such as optimal routes, delivery times, and factors that affect transportation efficiency. Machine learning techniques can be applied to learn from historical data and make predictions about future logistics operations.

**Route Optimization:** AI algorithms optimize transportation routes based on multiple factors, including distance, traffic conditions, fuel efficiency, delivery time windows, and vehicle capacities. By considering these variables, the algorithms can generate optimal routes that minimize travel time, reduce fuel consumption, and improve overall efficiency in delivering food products.

**Real-time Monitoring:** AI systems monitor transportation operations in real time using data from various sources, such as GPS trackers, sensors, and IoT devices. This allows for proactive management of potential disruptions, such as traffic congestion or unexpected delays. Real-time monitoring enables adjustments to routes and schedules, ensuring timely delivery and minimizing delays.

**Demand and Supply Matching:** AI algorithms can analyze demand patterns, production schedules, and inventory levels to optimize the allocation of resources and match supply with demand. By considering customer demands, delivery constraints, and available inventory, the algorithms can optimize logistics operations to ensure efficient order fulfillment and reduce stockouts or excess inventory.

**Load Optimization:** AI algorithms optimize the loading of vehicles by considering factors such as product types, weight, volume, and delivery sequence. By intelligently arranging products within the vehicles, the algorithms maximize space utilization, minimize the risk of product damage, and improve delivery efficiency.

**Predictive Maintenance:** AI can monitor the health and condition of logistics vehicles and equipment using sensor data and historical maintenance records. By analyzing this data, AI algorithms can predict maintenance needs and schedule proactive maintenance activities. Predictive maintenance reduces the risk of breakdowns, improves vehicle reliability, and minimizes disruptions in the supply chain.

## LEVERAGING IOT IN FOOD SUPPLY CHAIN MANAGEMENT

Over the years, more attention and activity in supply chain management generally have been noted. Compared to other supply chains, the food supply chain is more complicated and difficult to manage since it has been involved in crucial food conditions like quality of the food, safety issues, and its freshness. The present emerging problem for managing the supply chain is to fulfill the rising customer expectations for food quality as technological advancements alter society, the economy, and consumer behavior. The maintenance of good food quality, which includes other food qualities like safety and freshness, happens to be the most crucial indicator of the effectiveness of the food supply chain. The enhancement of food quality and safety is driven by integrating the food supply chain with quality assurance models and by understanding that food supply chain traceability would ensure food quality, safety, and customer happiness \[[11](#9789815274349-C7.html#r11)\].

### Introduction to IoT in the Context of Food Supply Chain

One of the most popular innovative technologies now being used in a variety of industrial areas is the Internet of Things (IoT). IoT refers to a collection of physically interconnected digital objects that are used to sense, monitor, and manage supply chain processes. It is observed that over the last ten years, efforts in supply chain research have focused on the Internet of Things. It is realistic to suppose that each food firm will evaluate a variety of variables when deciding whether to implement IoT in its own food supply chain management \[[12](#9789815274349-C7.html#r12)\].

### IoT-enabled Smart Sensors for Real-time Monitoring

IoT-enabled smart sensors are very much useful for day-to-day applications. These devices are generally termed smart appliances in real time. There are different areas where these smart appliances can be used. This is all because of the smart sensors that have been enabled with IoT. This is what we call the Industrial Revolution 4.0, which includes smart homes, smart cities, smart appliances, smart wearables for health monitoring, real-time transport management, object tracking in space and logistics, smart diagnosis models, etc \[[13](#9789815274349-C7.html#r13)\].

In smart cities, the real-time food sector is closely monitored, analyzed, and managed by IoT technology \[[14](#9789815274349-C7.html#r14)\]. The implementation of IoT technologies in the food sector enables effective and data-driven operations, enhancing sustainability, productivity, and quality control. The following are some ways that IoT systems assist smart cities in managing and monitoring the food business in real time:

**Smart Inventory Management:** IoT sensors can be used to continuously track the stock levels of food items. These sensors can monitor variables like temperature, humidity, and expiration dates, enabling better inventory management, waste reduction, and supply chain efficiency \[[15](#9789815274349-C7.html#r15)\].

**Quality Assurance and Security:** IoT devices may continuously monitor and gather information on a variety of variables that affect the nutritional value of food, such as relative humidity, temperature, and storage circumstances. Automated warnings can be set off whenever conditions deviate from ideal ones, enabling prompt remedial action to uphold the quality and safety of food requirements.

**Remote Process Monitoring:** IoT technology makes it possible to remotely monitor the manufacturing of food. Information about variables like humidity, pressure, temperature, and operational efficiency can be gathered by sensors and connected devices. The proactive maintenance and optimization of production processes are made possible by the real-time analysis of this data to spot any problems or inefficiencies \[[16](#9789815274349-C7.html#r16)\].

**Optimization of the Supply Chain and Traceability:** IoT technology enables seamless traceability in the food supply chain and supply chain optimization. The journey of food products from scratch to the table can be monitored and recorded using sensors that have RFID tags and blockchain-based technologies.

**Consumer Engagement and Personalization:** By offering individualized recommendations, nutritional data, and real-time information on food availability, IoT devices can improve the consumer experience. For instance, smart refrigerators may keep an eye on the contents and expiry dates of food products and offer recipe or shopping list recommendations depending on the stock of ingredients.

**Energy Conservation and Sustainability:** IoT systems enable the food business to optimize energy use. Connected devices can track energy consumption and spot places where energy efficiency can be improved, cutting down on waste and reducing emissions. IoT platforms may also analyze data to find trends and patterns that aid in making decisions that will encourage sustainable practices \[[17](#9789815274349-C7.html#r17)\].

Tracking and traceability using IoT devices:

IoT devices are essential for improving monitoring and tracing in supply chains because they help companies increase productivity, guarantee the quality and safety of their products, cut waste, and build consumer trust by giving them accurate information about the goods they buy.

Real-time data can be collected and transmitted by IoT devices, allowing for the tracking of product movement and status across the supply chain, as shown in Fig. ([**6**](#9789815274349-C7.html#f6)). IoT devices help with tracking and traceability in the following ways:

![](Images/9789815274349-C7-F6.jpg)**Fig. (6))**  
Tracking and traceability using IoT. 

**Supply Chain Visibility:** IoT-enabled tracking solutions give supply chain participants real-time visibility into the whereabouts and status of products. Businesses can track the flow of goods and spot delays or bottlenecks \[[18](#9789815274349-C7.html#r18), [19](#9789815274349-C7.html#r19)\].

**Monitoring of Temperature and Environmental Conditions:** IoT sensors can keep an eye on temperature levels and other outside conditions when transporting and storing perishable items. This information makes sure that products are kept within specific temperature ranges and quality requirements. Automated warnings can be set up if the temperature differs from the appropriate range, enabling prompt steps to stop spoiling or degradation \[[18](#9789815274349-C7.html#r18), [20](#9789815274349-C7.html#r20)\].

**RFID Tags and Sensors:** RFID tags and sensors are frequently employed in tracking systems built on the Internet of Things \[[21](#9789815274349-C7.html#r21)\]. These tags and sensors convey information like location, temperature, humidity, and other pertinent data and can be connected to specific products, containers, or pallets. IoT devices continuously record and update this data as goods transit through the supply chain \[[22](#9789815274349-C7.html#r22)\].

### IoT-based Temperature and Humidity Control

Precision monitoring, self-regulation, remote connectivity, data-driven decision-making, and improved compliance are just a few advantages of IoT-based temperature and humidity management systems. These technologies boost operational effectiveness across a variety of industries, enhance product quality and safety, and consume less energy. Connected equipment and sensors are used by IoT-based humidification and temperature management systems to monitor and manage environmental conditions in a variety of applications, including facilities for cold storage, shipping, and manufacturing processes \[[23](#9789815274349-C7.html#r23), [24](#9789815274349-C7.html#r24)\]. IoT offers humidity as well as temperature control in the following ways:

Real-time monitoring: The IoT platform receives the sensor data collection in real time. This makes it possible to continuously monitor the humidity and temperature levels. Users can monitor the data, establish limits, and receive warnings or notifications if conditions fall outside the range that has been established by logging into the application using web interfaces or mobile applications.

Automated Control: IoT devices offer automated control of temperature and humidity conditions. The Internet of Things (IoT) platform can be combined with connected devices like heating and cooling systems, humidification devices, and dehumidifying or HVAC (Heating, Ventilation, and Air Conditioning) systems, as shown in Fig. ([**7**](#9789815274349-C7.html#f7)).

Remote Connectivity and Control: Remote accessibility and control is one benefit of IoT-based humidity and temperature control \[[25](#9789815274349-C7.html#r25)\]. Utilizing internet-connected devices, users may access the IoT platform from any place to monitor and modify settings.

Analysis of data and insights: Internet of Things (IoT) applications frequently have these features available. To find patterns, trends, and anomalies, information concerning humidity and temperature can be analyzed. These discoveries aid in control strategy optimization, issue detection, and energy efficiency enhancement.

Integration with Other Systems: To increase total efficiency and automation, IoT-based temperature and humidity management platforms can be interconnected with other systems. To ensure optimal storage conditions for perishable commodities, integration with inventory management systems, for instance, can set off alarms or actions based on inventory levels and longevity requirements.

![](Images/9789815274349-C7-F7.jpg)**Fig. (7))**  
Sensors and their Controls. 

IoT platforms record previous temperature and humidity data, enabling users to monitor and examine long-term patterns. Additionally, this information may be used to comply with legal obligations, such as those relating to temperature-sensitive goods or showing evidence of conformity when being audited.

## BLOCKCHAIN TECHNOLOGY IN FOOD SUPPLY CHAIN MANAGEMENT

Blockchain technology is a distributed ledger system \[[26](#9789815274349-C7.html#r26), [27](#9789815274349-C7.html#r27)\] that allows for safe and transparent transaction recording and digital asset management. Blockchain runs through a network of computers called nodes that collaboratively maintain and validate the integrity of the ledger, unlike traditional centralized databases that depend on a single institution for control and verification. The concept of distributed ledger technology lies at the heart of blockchain. It involves a decentralized network where each participant has access to a copy of the ledger, and changes are propagated through consensus mechanisms. As a result of this dispersed structure, no one entity is able to exert total control over the system, which promotes openness and trust among users.

### Role of Blockchain Technology in Food Supply Chain Management

When applied to food supply chain management, blockchain technology has several substantial benefits \[[28](#9789815274349-C7.html#r28)\]. Blockchain can revolutionize the way we track and control food transportation from farm to fork by exploiting its inherent properties of transparency, traceability, and security. Here are some key benefits:

**Transparency:** Every transaction and movement of food along the supply chain is recorded on a transparent and immutable blockchain. This transparency enables stakeholders, including consumers, to track the origin, processing, and distribution of food products.

**Traceability:** Blockchain allows for the seamless tracing of food products from their source to the final destination. Each step of the supply chain, from harvesting or production to packaging, transportation, and retail, can be recorded on the blockchain.

**Mitigation of Food Fraud and Counterfeiting:** Blockchain technology can help combat food fraud and counterfeiting by establishing an unalterable record of food transactions. By recording every step of the supply chain, including the details of suppliers, distributors, and certifications, blockchain enables easy verification of the authenticity and integrity of food products.

**Efficient Supply Chain Management:** Blockchain's decentralized and transparent nature enables real-time visibility and information sharing among supply chain participants. This improves coordination, reduces delays, and eliminates paperwork or manual reconciliation processes.

**Food Safety and Quality Assurance:** Blockchain technology can contribute to ensuring food safety and quality throughout the supply chain. By recording relevant data such as temperature, humidity, and handling procedures at each stage, blockchain helps identify critical control points and potential risks that may impact food safety.

**Consumer Trust and Engagement:** With access to transparent and reliable information about the food they consume, consumers can make more informed choices. Blockchain empowers consumers to verify claims related to organic certifications, fair trade practices, or sustainable sourcing.

### Transparency and Traceability through Blockchain

One of the primary benefits of integrating blockchain technology in food supply chain management is increased transparency and traceability \[[29](#9789815274349-C7.html#r29)\]. The decentralized and irreversible ledger of blockchain provides a trustworthy and auditable record of every transaction and movement of food goods, allowing stakeholders to track and trace food from its origin to the consumer \[[30](#9789815274349-C7.html#r30)\]. The following characteristics of blockchain ensure transparency and traceability:

**Transparent Recordkeeping:** Blockchain creates a transparent and shared ledger accessible to all participants involved in the food supply chain, including farmers, manufacturers, distributors, retailers, regulators, and consumers. Every transaction, such as harvesting, processing, packaging, quality testing, and distribution, is recorded on the blockchain in chronological order.

**Immutable Transaction History:** Once recorded on the blockchain, data cannot be altered or tampered with, ensuring the integrity of the information. This immutability feature prevents fraudulent activities and unauthorized modifications to the records. Stakeholders can rely on the accuracy and authenticity of the information, leading to increased trust among participants and consumers.

**End-to-End Traceability:** End-to-end traceability of food goods is enabled by blockchain technology, allowing stakeholders to track and verify their origin, quality, and treatment at each level of the supply chain. Every transaction recorded on the blockchain includes relevant information such as product details, batch numbers, certifications, timestamps, and the identities of the parties involved.

**Supplier Verification and Compliance:** Blockchain facilitates supplier verification and compliance management by securely storing information about suppliers, certifications, and regulatory requirements. With blockchain, stakeholders can easily verify the authenticity and compliance of suppliers, ensuring adherence to ethical sourcing, sustainability, and quality standards.

**Consumer Empowerment:** Blockchain's transparency and traceability empower consumers to make informed choices about the food they purchase. By scanning QR codes or accessing digital platforms linked to the blockchain, consumers can access detailed information about the product's origin, production methods, ingredients, and safety certifications.

### Blockchain-based smart contracts for secure transactions

Blockchain technology, combined with smart contracts \[[31](#9789815274349-C7.html#r31)\], offers a powerful solution for secure transactions within the food supply chain. Smart contracts are self-executing agreements with predefined rules encoded on the blockchain. When applied to food supply chain management, blockchain-based smart contracts bring several benefits for secure and efficient transactions.

## INTEGRATION OF AI, IOT, AND BLOCKCHAIN IN FOOD SUPPLY CHAIN MANAGEMENT

Integrating AI, IoT, and blockchain technologies can bring numerous benefits and advancements across various industries. When applied to food supply chain management specifically, the integration of these technologies offers several important advantages, such as enhanced transparency, reduced food fraud, real-time monitoring of product conditions, and quick response to supply chain disruptions \[[32](#9789815274349-C7.html#r32), [33](#9789815274349-C7.html#r33)\].

There have been several successful pilot projects and initiatives that demonstrate the potential of these technologies. Here are a few notable success stories:

**IBM Food Trust:** IBM Food Trust is a blockchain-based platform that aims to enhance transparency and traceability in the food supply chain. It enables participants to track food products from farm to table, providing visibility into the origin, processing, and transportation of the products. The platform has been utilized by major companies such as Walmart, Nestlé, and Carrefour to improve food safety, reduce waste, and enhance consumer trust.

**Walmart and IBM's Mango Pilot:** In collaboration with IBM, Walmart conducted a pilot project that utilized blockchain, IoT, and AI technologies to trace the journey of mangoes from farm to store shelves. IoT sensors were used to monitor temperature and humidity during transportation, while blockchain recorded data at each stage. AI algorithms analyzed the data to identify potential issues and improve the supply chain efficiency.

**Alibaba's Blockchain-based Supply Chain Platform:** Alibaba, the Chinese e-commerce giant, launched a blockchain-based platform called “Food Trust Framework” to enhance food safety and traceability. The platform integrates IoT devices to collect data on product quality, storage conditions, and logistics. Blockchain technology ensures the immutability and transparency of the recorded data, while AI algorithms analyze the data for quality control and risk assessment.

**TE-Food:** TE-Food is a blockchain-based food traceability platform that leverages IoT and AI technologies. It tracks the movement of food products from farm to table, collecting data from various IoT devices such as barcodes, RFID tags, and sensors. The data is recorded on the blockchain, ensuring transparency and traceability. AI algorithms analyze the data to provide insights into food safety, quality control, and supply chain optimization. TE-Food has been implemented in several countries, including Vietnam and Hungary, to enhance food traceability and prevent fraud.

**LLamasoft:** LLamasoft incorporates AI, IoT, and blockchain technologies within its supply chain software solutions to enhance capabilities and provide additional value to its customers. AI is used in LLamasoft's software to leverage advanced analytics and algorithms for various applications. AI enables demand forecasting and planning, optimizing inventory levels, analyzing transportation routes, and identifying patterns or anomalies in supply chain data. By utilizing AI, LLamasoft's software can provide intelligent insights and recommendations to improve decision-making and enhance overall supply chain performance.

## CHALLENGES AND RESEARCH DIRECTIONS

The integration of blockchain, IoT, and AI in the food supply chain offers numerous research opportunities. Here are some potential directions for future studies in this field:

**Scalability and Performance:** Investigating ways to improve the scalability and performance of blockchain networks is a crucial research direction. As the volume of data generated by IoT devices increases, ensuring that blockchain networks can handle large-scale data transactions efficiently is essential.

**Privacy and Security:** Ensuring the privacy and security of data in the integrated system is a critical concern. Future research can focus on developing robust encryption methods, data anonymization techniques, and secure access control mechanisms. Privacy-preserving AI algorithms that can analyze encrypted data and extract meaningful insights without compromising sensitive information can be explored.

**Interoperability and Standards:** Investigating interoperability challenges and developing standardized protocols for communication and data exchange between IoT devices, blockchain networks, and AI systems is essential.

**Data Fusion and Analytics:** Exploring advanced data fusion techniques that integrate data from various IoT devices and other data sources can provide comprehensive insights into the food supply chain. AI algorithms can be further developed to extract actionable information from integrated data sets, enabling real-time decision-making, predictive analytics, and optimization of supply chain operations.

## CONCLUSION

In conclusion, the integration of AI, IoT, and blockchain technologies in food supply chain management brings significant benefits and advancements to the industry. By leveraging these technologies, companies can achieve enhanced traceability, improved food safety, increased operational efficiency, and greater transparency throughout the supply chain. AI enables data-driven insights and predictive capabilities, optimizing various aspects of the supply chain, such as demand forecasting, inventory management, and risk identification. IoT devices provide real-time monitoring of critical parameters, ensuring optimal conditions for food products and facilitating end-to-end traceability. Blockchain technology offers a decentralized and tamper-resistant ledger, ensuring transparency, trust, and accountability by recording and verifying each transaction and movement of food products.
